Completion of the proof of Theorem 1.2. The shooting construction gives bare couplings βN → ∞.
The prescribed lattice spacings satisfy aN = L−N → 0, and the source construction in Section 9
supplies the positive field renormalizations BN for the standard nearest-neighbour O(3) model. The
terminal kinetic coupling is the fixed number H at unit physical block length. Proposition 10.2
establishes convergence of the renormalized correlations and finite lists of smeared fields, with
infinite volume taken first or simultaneously along the specified torus exhaustion. Proposition 10.3
gives their temperedness and Euclidean symmetries.
    Proposition 11.3 reconstructs the local, unitary relativistic theory. Its full Hamiltonian gap is
Proposition 11.4, and Proposition 11.5 proves that a nonzero vacuum complement and a nonvanishing

                                                       94
connected fourth correlation survive in this same continuum limit. In particular the physical gap is
positive and finite at the fixed scale of the construction. Every limiting observable uses the field
renormalizations already prescribed from the nearest-neighbour model; the blocking observations
are integrated exactly and introduce no additional microscopic physical fields. The construction
adds no symmetry-breaking mass term.


Part II
Canonical scaling along all diverging couplings
12     Trajectories with a bounded bare offset
The construction above chooses the microscopic coupling by prescribing its value after the final
blocking step. To treat every sufficiently large microscopic coupling, we instead allow a bounded
displacement from an explicit reference sequence. We shall prove that all these trajectories remain
admitted. We shall also identify which displacements give the same effective density at every
fixed layer as the microscopic depth tends to infinity. This second conclusion requires a summable
sensitivity estimate for the error in the kinetic increment.

12.1      Common reference scales and canonical increments
We keep the observations and representations of Sections 3–5. In particular, L is a fixed sufficiently
large power of two, and
          log L                                                                            −1/2
     γ=         ,   Hj = H + γj,       mj = ⌈(log Hj )2 ⌉,    pj = (log Hj )P0 ,    tj = Hj       pj .
           2π
A depth-N trajectory starts at bN = β and runs through layers N, N − 1, . . . , 0. Its precise coupling
is admitted at layer j when bj ∈ [Hj /2, 2Hj ], and strict admission means that both inequalities
are strict. The reference scales, rather than bj , determine the geometric thresholds and masks
throughout a comparison.
    There are three trajectory types, denoted by P ∈ {r, +, −}. Type r uses only ordinary obser-
vations of side L. Type + or − uses first the inclined observation of side 5L in Section 4, with
respective rotation                              !

and then uses ordinary observations. A history records the observations already made, including
their coordinate frames. In a comparison we identify the frames of the layer under consideration
and use the same ordinary observation in the remaining common steps.
    We use the bulk formulas and their compatible finite-period representations. Compatibility
means that every observation descends to the corresponding quotient lattice, while admission of
a period requires its shortest nonzero translation to satisfy the lower bound in Section 3.1. The
comparisons below use common reference scales and the common refinements and zero padding
of Section 3. They apply to bulk lists and simultaneously to the finite-period lists whenever the
periods being compared are compatible. In particular, one may fix a common terminal period and
compare the resulting common periods at all fixed bottom layers.
    Recall briefly what the discrepancy measures. A retained density has the form (3.29): it contains
the precise kinetic coupling, a finite vector of canonical polynomial coefficient lists, regular local

                                                  95
errors, and a covering sum for configurations with large bond differences. The regular errors are
bounded with eight derivatives on their graph domains; the covering activities are bounded by a
sum over supports meeting a site. Their caps are

Proof. The initialization in Section 6 has zero canonical coefficients and zero regular errors; its

starting from zero therefore fixes the sequences αhP , κPh independently of the precise coupling and of

in Section 6; Lemma 6.1 identifies their common first limit as −γ. Reflection interchanges the two
inclined prescriptions and preserves these scalar extractions, so their scalar sequences agree. This
does not identify their coefficient tensors in a fixed coordinate frame.
    Equation (12.6) follows from (5.2) with the simultaneous bulk and period convention. To verify

the bulk raw lists, with the factor Ct−2
                                       j−1 in Section 5.7. The same bulk corrections are then used to
normalize every finite-period output. Their regular reset costs Ct2j−1 times this difference, cancelling
the extraction factor; the other reset discrepancies are the history and coupling terms already
present in (5.2). Missing bulk corrections on a given period keep their complete winding records and
the spare support exponent, as in the period argument of Section 5.7; their off-mask parts retain
the covering estimates. These operations introduce only the fixed normalization multipliers already
allowed before choosing L. Thus each output discrepancy is bounded by quj +CL poly(Hj )ϵh +ej |λj |,
with a common contraction coefficient q below ρ after the triangular weights and large parameters
are chosen with spare width. Taking their maximum proves the first family inequality; the coupling
inequality uses the bulk term directly. The history and kernel conclusions are Proposition 4.3. The
spare width in (12.7) is the choice made in Proposition 6.3. The uniform oH (1) terms in the step
estimates can be absorbed into a fixed upper contraction bound q strictly below ρ; increasing H
later does not change either chosen rate.

12.2    Summable sensitivity of the kinetic error
The coefficient ej in (12.6) tends to zero but is not summable over layers. For matching trajectories
from their behavior at large j, we need finer information about the noncanonical increment ∆j . The
raw-error estimate used in the proof of the exact step supplies it.

                                                   97
Lemma 12.2 (Sensitivity of the extracted kinetic error). For two admitted compatible representation
families, including their prescribed bulk lists, on a common ordinary step with common reference
scales, the discrepancies in Proposition 12.1 satisfy
                        |∆2,j − ∆1,j | ≤ CL Hj−1.01 (uj + |λj |) + CL poly(Hj )ϵh .                   (12.8)
The constant is uniform over the bulk prescriptions and their compatible admitted finite-period
representations, once H is sufficiently large.
Proof. Before the constant and affine terms of the regular remainder are extracted, let Rj∆ denote
the layer-(j − 1), seven-derivative norm of the difference of its two bulk raw lists. The Taylor
comparison proved in Section 5.7 gives the estimate [28, Equation (5.39)], with the improved
coefficient q1 = C/L2 + oH (1) established there for S 2 :
                  Rj∆ ≤ q1 δj∆ + CL poly(mj , pj )δj (ϵh + λg ) + CL g 4.5 (uj + ϵh + λg ).           (12.9)

Here δj∆ is the unnormalized bulk input regular-error discrepancy; g may be taken comparable
           −1/2                                                                               −1/2
to either bi,j ; and λg ≤ CL |λj |/Hj . By (12.2), δj∆ ≤ CL Hj−2.05 uj , while g ≤ CL Hj           in the
common bands.
   The affine coefficient is the linear second-derivative functional (3.24), evaluated in the bulk. Once
the coordinate frames and reference domains have been identified, this is the same functional on
both raw lists; it does not change with the free kinetic kernel. The extraction estimate in Section 5.7
therefore gives
                                 |∆2,j − ∆1,j | ≤ Ct−2    ∆
                                                    j−1 Rj ≤ CL Hj Rj .
                                                                       ∆
                                                                                                  (12.10)
There is no support-cardinality loss in this estimate: a unit affine direction is bounded by the
polynomially weighted directions in (3.21), so its two derivative slots cost only t−2
                                                                                    j−1 . The seven
derivatives in the discrepancy norm are more than sufficient. The final reset of the canonical
prefactors has zero affine Hessian, as verified in Section 5.7, and hence contributes no additional
kinetic extraction. Bulk extraction also explains the uniformity in the period: missing or folded
corrections remain in the winding lists instead of changing ∆j . Equation (12.10) uses the bulk
raw-list discrepancy, which is bounded using ubulk
                                               j   ≤ uj from (12.1). The inclusion of this bulk term
is essential when some labels wind or disappear on a finite period.
    Multiplying (12.9) by CL Hj bounds its nonhistory terms by
                    CL (Hj−1.05 + Hj−1.25 )uj + CL (log Hj )C Hj−2.05 + Hj−2.25 |λj |.
                                                                                   


Any remaining fixed logarithmic powers are absorbed by the strict power margins when H is
increased. In particular this is bounded by CL Hj−1.01 (uj + |λj |). The history terms can be bounded
by CL poly(Hj )ϵh , proving (12.8). The argument uses the raw-error comparison for S 2 from
Section 5.7, rather than an application of an O(4) step theorem to the present model.

                                                         99
Lemma 12.4 (Retuning at an arbitrary depth). After increasing H once more, let an ordinary
trajectory have any depth N ≥ 1 and bare offset |s| ≤ R. For every integer K ≥ 1 there is
sK ∈ [−R′ , R′ ] such that the ordinary trajectory of depth K with bare coupling ϑK + sK has exactly
the same terminal coupling b0 .

Proof. For every trajectory in Proposition 12.3, summing (12.15) to zero gives

                                     |b0 − H − s| ≤ Aabs + oH (1).                              (12.17)

Let TK (t) be the terminal coupling of the ordinary depth-K trajectory started at ϑK + t. Proposi-
tion 12.3 gives strict admission for the whole interval [−R′ , R′ ]. The continuity argument in the
proof of Proposition 6.2 therefore makes TK continuous on this interval: the reference thresholds
are fixed, and the exact integrations and representation maps are continuous in the precise coupling
on each finite admitted run. Take H so that the uniform oH (1) in (12.17) has absolute value less
than one. By (12.13),

                     TK (−R′ ) ≤ H − R′ + Aabs + 1 < H − R − Aabs − 1 ≤ b0 ,
                       TK (R′ ) ≥ H + R′ − Aabs − 1 > H + R + Aabs + 1 ≥ b0 .

The intermediate value theorem supplies sK . No monotonicity or uniqueness of the shooting map is
needed.

12.4    Matching at each fixed layer
Retuning gives an exact equality of terminal couplings at finite depths. A different comparison
identifies limits without such a retuning. Its hypothesis compensates the transient displacement AP
associated with the choice of initial observation.

                                                         101
13     Volume estimates uniform in the bare offset
The trajectories admitted in Proposition 12.3 have terminal couplings in a fixed band, but generally
do not end at H. We now extend the finite-volume estimates of Section 8 to this entire family.
The argument first matches two trajectories at their common terminal coupling, then transfers
a rectangular partition-function test from a retuned reference depth to every finer cutoff. The
resulting bounds will allow local field comparisons on fixed tori to pass to the plane.

13.1    Positivity at finite coupling
We first record the elementary positivity needed to use component moments as measures. All torus
partition functions retain the normalization in (1.1): normalized area measure at each site, and
each unoriented nearest-neighbor bond counted once.
Lemma 13.1 (Finite-coupling moments and normalization). For every sufficiently large fixed β,
the periodic plane law µβ exists and is translation and O(3) invariant. Every component moment
                                      n
                                      Y
                                Eµβ         qxirr ,      xr ∈ Z2 ,        ir ∈ {1, 2, 3},
                                      r=1

is nonnegative. If
                                                                                                 2
                                                                                            x |x| Cβ (x)
                                                                                        P
                                                             X
                 Cβ (x) = Eµβ [q01 qx1 ],             χβ =          Cβ (x),     ξβ2 =                      ,
                                                                                              4χβ
                                                             x∈Z2

then both sums converge absolutely and 0 < χβ , ξβ < ∞, where ξβ is the positive square root. More
precisely, Cβ (0) = 1/3 and, for each nearest-neighbor vector e,
                                                  β −7β
                                                Cβ (e) ≥
                                                    e    > 0.                                    (13.1)
                                                  9
Proof. Proposition 2.1 gives the periodic plane limit. The symmetries of its torus approximants
pass to that limit; in particular each component has mean zero. Apply (2.2) to q01 , qx1 on arbitrarily
large tori at this fixed β, and then pass to the plane. It gives
                               |Cβ (x)| ≤ C(1 + β + |x|)p exp[−c|x|/X(β)].
The two sums in the statement therefore converge absolutely.
    For positivity, expand each factor exp(β 3i=1 qxi qyi ) in its componentwise power series on a finite
                                               P

torus. The series of absolute values is integrable, since the spins are bounded and the torus has
finitely many edges. Every integrated term, including any prescribed component insertion, factors
into one-site monomial integrals. Such an integral vanishes if one coordinate exponent is odd and
is nonnegative if all are even. All expansion coefficients are nonnegative. Division by the positive
partition function, followed by the periodic limit, proves the asserted moment positivity.
    Consider one edge {0, e} on a torus with sides at least four. Exactly seven distinct bonds meet
its two endpoints. Delete these bonds and denote the resulting partition function by Z del . In the
expansion of the numerator for q01 qe1 , retain the term βq01 qe1 from the chosen edge and the constant
terms from the other six incident bonds, leaving all nonincident expansions unrestricted. The
isolated endpoint integrals each equal 1/3, so this contribution is βZ del /9. Termwise nonnegativity
bounds the full numerator from below by this value. Since the deleted interaction is at most 7β
pointwise, Z ≤ e7β Z del . This proves (13.1), also after passing to the plane. Internal symmetry
gives Cβ (0) = 1/3. Nonnegativity now gives χβ ≥ 1/3 and a strictly positive second-moment
numerator.

13.2    Endpoint comparisons with a varying terminal coupling
The reference scale at the endpoint remains H throughout. Thus the thresholds are always
                                                                                            1/4
t0 = H −1/2 p0 , the regular-error cap is δ0 = H −2.05 , and the covering cap is w0 = exp(−p0 ). These
quantities are not replaced by functions of the precise terminal coupling.
Lemma 13.2 (Ordinary endpoint laws throughout the terminal band). Fix a finite covering-cap
multiplier and C0 > 0 before choosing H sufficiently large. Let a trajectory obtained from the
microscopic Gibbs law by the exact ordinary observations satisfy the admission and cap conditions
of Section 3, with terminal coupling b0 ∈ [H/2, 2H]. On a standard terminal axis torus of bounded
aspect, with the lower bounds and divisibilities of Section 7, its scalar-stripped endpoint density is an
admissible base for Proposition 7.1 and Corollary 7.5, with constants uniform in the trajectory, its
depth, its period, and b0 in this band. In particular, every endpoint bond obeys
                                                                              2
                                          µ{|qx − qy | > t0 /C0 } ≤ e−cHt0                         (13.2)
for a corresponding c > 0. The integrated comparison also permits complex comparison weights whose
support-hit envelope obeys the chosen multiplier of the covering cap, as in its original statement.
Proof. The definition in Section 3.4, specifically (3.31), already allows a precise coupling in [H/2, 2H].
An actual observed law is positive because its density is the integral of the positive microscopic
density against positive normalized observations. The ordinary observations on the two sides of
a retained seam are independent and are exchanged by reflection. The verification at the end of
Section 6 therefore supplies link reflection positivity without any condition b0 = H.
    The quantitative estimates used in the integrated comparison have the same uniformity. In
Lemma 7.2, group the kinetic rows with their precise coupling b0 . The omitted tails cost at most
2H times the exponentially small kernel tail, and the canonical contributions remain bounded
by CL (Ht40 + t20 ) per anchor. In Lemma 7.4, the lower and upper energy bounds use respectively
b0 ≥ H/2 and b0 ≤ 2H; their energy scale is still Ht20 = p20 . The sampling, masks, complete supports,
and inventory constraints have the same reference thresholds and caps. The chessboard argument
uses only the seam reflection positivity and the even tile counts, both just checked. These are
precisely the hypotheses of Proposition 7.1. The disseminated-event proof of Corollary 7.5 uses the
same remainder and partition-function bounds and the lower kinetic bound b0 ≥ H/2. It therefore
gives (13.2) uniformly.

Proposition 13.5 (Uniform volume comparison and slab decay). Fix K0 , M0 as in Lemma 13.4.
There exist C, c > 0, independent of N ≥ K0 , |s| ≤ R, and k ≥ 0, such that the following hold for
β = ϑN + s.
   Let (u1 , u2 ) ∈ A and (n, w) = (u1 , u2 )2k M0 LN . If a bounded local observable F has support
contained in a rectangle of time extent at most n/2 and spatial extent at most w/2, then
                                                                         k
                               |µβ;n,w (F ) − µβ (F )| ≤ C∥F ∥∞ e−c2 .                         (13.8)

The same bound holds when time is closed by any integer spatial translation, provided the support
has a lift satisfying these two extent bounds. The constant is uniform in the closing translation.
    If two bounded local plane observables F, G have supporting slabs separated by d ≥ 0 transfer
edges, in either microscopic axial direction, then
                                                                             N
                           | Covµβ (F, G)| ≤ ∥F ∥∞ ∥G∥∞ e−(log 2)d/(M0 L ) .                   (13.9)

For complex observables the covariance uses F G.

Proof. For clarity we specify which parts of the transfer proof are independent of the chosen
trajectory. The nearest-neighbor operator Kw of (8.4) is positive semidefinite and has strictly
positive kernel for every β ≥ 0. Its largest eigenvalue is simple; let Tw = Kw /λ1 (w), let Pw be the
projection onto its normalized positive ground state Ωw , and write

                                 sj (w) = Tr(Twj − Pw ),        j ≥ 1.

Lemma 8.2 and Proposition 8.3 use only this positivity and the rectangular test. They give
                                                                 k
                                     ∆β (2k n, 2k w) ≤ 64−1 2−2

from each test in (13.6). Applied at the auxiliary aspects of (13.5), this implies
                                              k                              k
                            sn/2 (w) ≤ Ce−c2 ,          sw/2 (2n) ≤ Ce−c2 ,

where the second expression refers to transfer in the spatial direction.
    An insertion on a slab of r ≥ 1 steps has normalized operator AF whose kernel is bounded in
absolute value by ∥F ∥∞ Kwr before normalization. Thus |AF f | ≤ ∥F ∥∞ Twr |f | and ∥AF ∥ ≤ ∥F ∥∞ ,
also for complex F . For r = 0, AF is multiplication by F and obeys the same norm bound. For
r ≤ n/2, separating the ground-state projection in the transfer trace gives (8.10):

                           |µβ;n,w (F ) − ⟨Ωw , AF Ωw ⟩| ≤ 2∥F ∥∞ sn/2 (w).

Use this first to compare (n, w) with (2n, w), and then, after interchanging the coordinates, to
                                                                                                  k
compare (2n, w) with (2n, 2w). The two preceding excited-trace estimates give an error C∥F ∥∞ e−c2 .
Summing over all further simultaneous doublings proves (13.8); the limit is µβ by Proposition 2.1.
Bounded measurable insertions are covered as in the proof of Theorem 8.4: finite-support marginals
have a uniform density bound at this fixed β, and approximation in product area measure extends
the identification of the local limit.

   For a closing shift h, its unitary Uh commutes with Tw and fixes Ωw . Place the seam outside the
supporting slab. The denominator and numerator are respectively

                                   Tr(Twn Uh ),    Tr(AF Twn−r Uh ).

Their remainders after the ground-state contribution have absolute values at most sn (w) and
∥F ∥∞ sn−r (w), uniformly in h. The reference tests ensure sn (w) < 1/2. Division thus changes the
cylinder expectation by at most 4∥F ∥∞ sn/2 (w), proving the shifted assertion.
    Finally set n0 = M0 LN . The square test gives, at all circumferences w = 2k n0 ,

                                      ∥Twd − Pw ∥ ≤ e−(log 2)d/n0

by (8.9). For ordered slab insertions the cylinder covariance is

                                     ⟨Ωw , AF (Twd − Pw )AG Ωw ⟩.

The insertion norm bounds prove (13.9) on these cylinders. Their local limit is the periodic plane
law by the half-period trace estimates and the torus comparison already proved. Passing to that
limit proves the plane estimate. Interchanging coordinates gives the other axial direction.

13.5    Common physical tori for the inclined schemes
The shifted closing condition has a concrete role: it puts the ordinary and inclined embeddings on
the same physical square tori. We spell out the integer geometry to fix both the length factor and
the finite list of aspects used above.

Lemma 13.6 (Inclined periods). Let Mk = 2k M0 . Embed an ordinary depth-N run with spacing
                                                                                  −1
L−N and axes the identity, and a type ± run with spacing a = L−N /5 and axes O±      . The square
physical torus of side Mk has ordinary microscopic periods Mk LN e1 and Mk LN e2 . For type + its
microscopic periods, in (space, time) coordinates, are

                             v1 = Mk LN (3, 4),      v2 = Mk LN (−4, 3).                       (13.10)

They are equivalent to spatial circumference 25Mk LN and time height Mk LN , closed by the spatial
shift 7Mk LN . The reflected type has the same circumference and height, with shift −7Mk LN . These
periods are compatible with the initial inclined blocking and the subsequent ordinary blockings, and
have terminal square periods Mk .
    Let FN be any bounded observable whose physical support in the chosen embedding is contained

                                                                                           −1
Proof. The columns of O+ are (3, 4)/5 and (−4, 3)/5. Multiplication of (13.10) by (L−N /5)O+

                     3v1 − 4v2 = (25Mk LN , 0),         v1 − v2 = (7Mk LN , Mk LN )

                                                  107
ordinary steps yield square periods Mk . The fixed divisibilities of M0 and the terminal lower bound

     A compact physical support of diameter D has microscopic coordinate extents at most CD/a ≤

sufficiently large k, these are at most half the time height Mk LN and half the spatial circumference
25Mk LN . The same assertion for the ordinary frame uses aspect (1, 1). Thus the two aspects in A
and the shifted estimate of Proposition 13.5 prove the final claim.


14     Continuum fields at a fixed bare-coupling offset
The density comparison of Proposition 12.5 leaves one real parameter, the limiting offset of the
bare coupling. We now construct the field hierarchy at each offset and compare the three blocking
prescriptions in common physical coordinates. The last part of the section controls the full two-point
measure, including its tails. This is what permits normalization by susceptibility and second-moment
length; convergence on compact tests alone would not suffice.
    Throughout, the parameters have the choices of Propositions 12.3 and 13.5. Write I = (−R, R),
and let a run of type P ∈ {r, +, −}, depth N , and offset s have bare coupling β = ϑN + s. Recall
the matching condition
                                     (s2 + AP2 ) − (s1 + AP1 ) −→ 0,                            (14.1)
for two sequences of runs whose depths tend to infinity. The discrepancies uj and λj are the
source-free density and precise-coupling discrepancies at layer j. By Proposition 12.5, both tend to
zero at each fixed layer under (14.1). All finite-period comparisons below use the same reference
thresholds and the common record lists, with zero padding, prescribed in Section 3.

14.1    Local sources and their normalization
We use the exact source representation of Section 9. Its additional regular records have positive
source degree and are anchored at a source site; their complete supports include every source and
spin argument. The covering records retain their mandatory inventories and complete supports. All
norms sum absolute power-series coefficients at source radius one. In particular, identifying source
variables or setting some of them to zero respects the bounds. The leading linear spin source is kept
separately from these records. Spin-independent terms of source degree at least two stay in their
supported regular records. There is no source-dependent volume scalar whose removal could change
a moment generating function.
    To distinguish a source cap from the offset radius R, write

regular differences measured through seven derivatives and absent records padded by zero. Write
these quantities as vjsrc,bulk and vjsrc,T , where T denotes a common terminal period followed through
the layers. In parallel with the density discrepancy (12.1), use

Proof. The initial source has zero additional regular and covering lists and has all support, normal-

inductively and renews the caps. Equation (14.3) is Equation (9.2). We spell out the simultaneous
use of the comparison proof, since the scalar is extracted in bulk and then used on every period.
    Before the final scalar division, the one-difference estimates in the proof of Proposition 9.1 give
an isolated degree-one transfer factor C/L + oH (1) and a higher-degree factor C/L2 + oH (1); these
are the discrepancy versions of Equations (9.6) and (9.5). The remaining regular terms and covering

period, using the dominating discrepancies uj and vjsrc . Extract c2,j − c1,j from the bulk degree-one

bulk coefficients. The extraction paragraph of the source proof controls the subtraction, off-mask
corrections, and winding compensations by fixed multiples of their coefficient bounds, with spare
support exponents paying for the declared winding records. Changing the scalar argument in an
already small positive-degree remainder has the additional source cap and is controlled by the spare
analytic radius. Thus these operations preserve the preceding contraction gains up to multipliers
fixed before the choice of L, using the already reserved support exponents. As in the closure of that
proof, choose L sufficiently large for these multipliers and then H sufficiently large for the remaining
allowances. The resulting common contraction is still qs < 1. The precise-coupling discrepancy
and history forcing are shared across the family. Taking its supremum after these normalization
estimates proves the first line of (14.4); the bulk extraction estimate proves the second line. This
is the simultaneous interpretation of Equation (9.3), rather than an inference from a finite-period
discrepancy alone. The source-free output and the source-independent bulk scalar are unchanged.
No terminal-coupling equality is a hypothesis of that proposition.

Lemma 14.2 and a bounded covering of the dilated, rotated boxes prove (14.11) in the plane.
    On a fixed inclined physical torus, the terminal volume is fixed. Absolute coefficient norms and
support-hit bounds bound the entire source numerator in that volume. The source-free normalizer is
uniformly bounded below after its bulk scalar has been removed: integrate all spins in one sufficiently
small spherical cap. There are then no bad bonds, the mandatory gas has empty inventory and
Ξ = 1, and the regular and kinetic exponents are bounded. Positivity of the complete source-free
density permits this restriction of the integral. The resulting bound can depend on the fixed terminal
volume and on H, but not on N or s. The inclined descendants of a terminal site remain within a
fixed distance of its center, and every physical unit box meets descendants of only boundedly many
sites. The preceding one-cell and covering argument now proves the asserted fixed-torus estimate.
Reflection positivity of inclined steps is not used.
    Finally consider a real linear combination Y of a finite list of field smearings. For even n,
expand components and integrate the absolute test coefficients against the nonnegative measures
in (14.11). A fixed bounded support can be covered by finitely many unit boxes, so E|Y |n ≤

exponential series converges for a sufficiently small positive argument. Hölder’s inequality gives the
same conclusion for the sum of the absolute values of the finite list. This proves the assertions for
signed as well as nonnegative tests.

14.3    Convergence at a limiting offset
The bounds just established allow us to pass from descendants of fixed coarse cells to arbitrary
compact tests. We first work on a fixed physical torus, where the density and source comparisons
control normalized integrals directly. The uniform volume estimate then identifies the plane limit.
Proposition 14.4 (Limits and comparison at fixed offsets). Let N → ∞ and s → s∗ with |s| ≤ R,
for a fixed type P . The fields (14.9) converge jointly in law and in all joint moments for every finite
list of continuous compactly supported vector tests in the plane. The analogous assertions hold for
continuous periodic tests on each fixed common physical torus. The limiting laws are determined by
their moments.
     Two such sequences satisfying (14.1) have the same limits in their specified embeddings. At every
order and component choice, the plane measures (14.10) converge on continuous compact tests and
in the strong topology of S ′ ((R2 )n ).
     Denote the ordinary limiting hierarchy at offset s ∈ I by Fs , including its moment-determined
joint laws. For either inclined type, the limit at offset s is

                        Fs+d ,        s, s + d ∈ I,       d = A+ − Ar = A− − Ar .               (14.13)

Proof. Fix a common physical torus and a layer j below the initial inclined step, if present. Its grid
has mesh L−j . Align the grids across the runs and use the same ordinary coordinate prescriptions
on their common tail. These alignments are allowed by the translation covariance of the blocking.

                                                       112
Translating a microscopic origin has, by microscopic translation invariance, the same effect on the
moment measures as a translation of size O(a). Lemma 14.3 and uniform continuity make this
residual error vanish on continuous tests.
    Assign a test’s value at a layer-j center to every microscopic descendant of that center. The
exact source multiplier for these cell tests is

Their product is (14.14); the area factor of the inclined first step is already included in a2 . Only a
fixed number of scalar factors occur, so this multiplier is bounded, bounded away from zero, and
compares between matched runs by Lemma 14.2.
    At this fixed layer the period and its number of sites are fixed. The coefficient norms for canonical
records sum their path coefficients; the masked regular norms bound the sums of their suprema;
and the covering norms bound the activity sums at a support hit. Proposition 12.5 therefore makes
the differences of the regular exponents and the absolutely convergent covering series tend to zero.
The free-history bounds give the same assertion for the kinetic kernels. Their masks and reference
thresholds are common, and bj remains in a fixed band. Thus the source-free exponents are bounded
and compare uniformly after their volume scalars have been discarded. The source-list assertion in
Lemma 14.2 gives the corresponding comparison of the positive-degree terms in absolute coefficient
norms.
    For a finite list of cell tests choose one sufficiently small complex polydisc in their source variables
so that all substituted arguments lie in a smaller source polydisc at layer j. The absolute series
bounds control perturbations of these arguments as the multiplier (14.14) varies. The source-free
denominators have a uniform positive lower bound by the same small-cap argument as in Lemma 14.3,
now at layer j. Since the removed volume scalars are source independent, they cancel in the ratios.
The normalized generating functions of the cell tests consequently differ by a quantity tending
uniformly to zero on this polydisc. Cauchy’s formula proves comparison of all fixed joint moments.
    Every microscopic descendant lies within CL−j of its layer-j center, for both cell geometries in
the embeddings (14.8). On the fixed torus, replacing a continuous test by these cell values therefore
has an error bounded by its modulus of continuity at CL−j . Telescoping a product of tests one
factor at a time and using (14.11) bounds the moment error by a constant times these moduli,
uniformly in depth. First let the depths tend to infinity at fixed j, and then let j → ∞. This proves
moment comparison for continuous tests. Comparing two arbitrary subsequences of one sequence
proves the Cauchy criterion, hence moment convergence.
    The factorial bounds of Lemma 14.3 imply tightness for each finite list of real field variables and
uniform integrability of every polynomial in them. Each subsequential law has the moments just
constructed and an exponential moment in a neighborhood of the origin. Its moment generating
function is analytic there and is determined by those moments; this determines the law. All
subsequential laws thus coincide, proving joint convergence in law on the fixed torus.
    For the plane, let exp(i rν=1 tν ϕP,N,s (fν )) be a characteristic function observable. Its modulus
                                P

is one regardless of B, and its microscopic support is the support of the smearings. For sufficiently

                                                          113
large k this support satisfies the lift and half-size conditions in Proposition 13.5, including the
shifted-period description in Lemma 13.6. That proposition gives
                                        P                             P                          k
                            Eplane ei     t ϕ(fν )
                                         ν ν         − Etorus(k) ei      t ϕ(fν )
                                                                        ν ν            ≤ Ce−c2 ,                   (14.15)
uniformly in the cutoff and offset. Plane tightness follows from the plane moment bound. Every
                                                                                 k
subsequential plane characteristic function is, for each fixed k, within Ce−c2 of the already
constructed fixed-torus limit. Letting k → ∞ identifies all subsequential plane laws and gives
their equality for matched runs. Plane uniform integrability then gives convergence of all joint
moments. Only bounded observables enter (14.15); no bound uniform in increasing inclined torus
size is needed.
    Finite sums of product tests are uniformly dense on a product of compact insertion-coordinate
sets. The component measures have uniformly bounded mass there by (14.11). The joint moment
convergence therefore gives convergence against every continuous compact test to a nonnegative
Radon measure. Summing unit boxes with the integrable weights (1 + |yi |2 )−2 gives, at fixed order

                                                                                          n

This proves temperedness and convergence on Schwartz tests by cutting off the tails.
    For completeness, this convergence is strong: it is uniform on each bounded subset B of Schwartz
space. The higher weighted seminorms bounded on B make the discarded tails tend to zero uniformly
in pn . On a fixed compact set, restrictions of B are uniformly bounded and equicontinuous, hence
have finite uniform-norm nets. Convergence for the finitely many net elements and the common
compact mass bound make the error uniform on B. Combining these two statements proves the
strong topology assertion.
    Finally, a type ± sequence with offset tending to s and an ordinary sequence with offset tending
to s + d satisfy (14.1). This proves (14.13).

14.4    The relative two-point measure
We next pass from local convergence to susceptibility and second-moment length. Write S2cut for
the first-component two-point measure of a run. In coordinates (y1 , z) = (y1 , y2 − y1 ), microscopic
translation invariance gives the exact factorization
                                                 X                                       X
          S2cut = αa ⊗ ν cut ,        αa = a2           δaU x ,       ν cut = B 2 a2           Cβ (x)δaU x .       (14.17)
                                                x∈Z2                                    x∈Z2

The first factor tends vaguely to Lebesgue measure. The second carries the susceptibility and length,
so we must control its mass at large relative separation.
Lemma 14.5 (Uniform two-point tails). There are constants C, c > 0, uniform over N ≥ K0 ,
|s| ≤ R, and the three types, such that for physical unit boxes with center separation T ,
                                              S2cut (Q × Q′ ) ≤ Ce−cT .                                            (14.18)
Consequently, for a unit box Qu centered at u,
                                                ν cut (Qu ) ≤ Ce−c|u| .                                            (14.19)
Along each convergent-offset sequence of Proposition 14.4, the measures ν cut converge vaguely, in
total mass, and in second moment to a finite nonnegative measure ν. Its tensor product with Lebesgue
measure is the limiting first-component two-point measure in the relative coordinates.

Proof. Let XQ and XQ′ be the first-component field sums over the two boxes. Internal symmetry

                            ∥XQ − TD (XQ )∥2L2 ≤ D−2 E|XQ |4 ≤ CD−2 .

The second moments of the other factors are bounded, so Cauchy–Schwarz shows that replacing

Choosing D = ec2 T /4 proves (14.18); a larger constant covers bounded separations.

14.5    Positive susceptibility and length
Proposition 14.6 (Canonical normalization at a fixed offset). For every s ∈ I, the quantities m(s)
and ℓ(s) in (14.20) are finite and strictly positive. For an ordinary sequence with N → ∞ and offset

                                 B 2 a2 χβ −→ m(s),       aξβ −→ ℓ(s).                      (14.21)
For an inclined sequence with limiting offset s and s, s + d ∈ I, the limits are m(s + d) and ℓ(s + d).

    The functions m, ℓ are continuous on I. At every order and component choice, evaluation of Fs
and of Gs on any fixed Schwartz test is continuous in s.

     To check normalization and uniqueness, let a translation-invariant hierarchy have relative two-

                                 ν ′ = b2 l2 (D1/l )∗ ν,      D1/l (z) = z/l.

For P = r this is Ψβ ; for P = ± it is the corresponding spatial rotation of the same canonical field.
    The limits of mcut and ℓcut are positive and finite. The scalar multipliers in (14.25) are therefore
bounded, and the varying dilations of a continuous compact test converge uniformly with supports
in a common compact set. The moment bound proves that this varying-test error tends to zero.
It also gives uniform box bounds for the normalized measures: the inverse dilation of a unit box
is covered by boundedly many old unit boxes. Factorial moments then give joint-law convergence
and uniform integrability just as in Proposition 14.4. The common weighted bound and finite-net
argument in that proposition give strong Schwartz convergence for the normalized measures as well.
    Finally let si → s in I. For any one of the scalar quantities m, ℓ or a fixed component Schwinger
evaluation of F or G, choose a depth Ni ≥ i at offset si for which the corresponding cutoff quantity
approximates its limit at si within 1/i. The convergence statements above apply to this sequence
because its offsets tend to s; they identify its limit with the quantity at s. This proves all the
asserted continuities without assuming uniform convergence in the offset in advance.
Lemma 14.7 (Normalization of the shooting hierarchy). The shooting hierarchy constructed in
Proposition 10.2 has finite positive susceptibility and finite positive second-moment length. Applying
the unique positive rescaling (14.22) to it is the limit of applying canonical susceptibility and length
normalization to its cutoff fields. These assertions also hold when its sufficiently large auxiliary
parameters are fixed independently of those used for the offset trajectories.
Proof. Use the shooting prescription’s own constants and physical mesh aN = L−N . Theorem 8.4
supplies its slab covariance estimate at scale LN , and Proposition 10.1 supplies uniform fourth
moments and all-order factorial bounds. The factorization (14.17) is an exact lattice identity, so
the proof of Lemma 14.5 applies with these constants. Proposition 10.2 then identifies the relative
measure and gives convergence of its mass and second moment. Equation (11.9) in the proof of
Proposition 11.5 gives a uniform positive two-point expectation on two separated terminal cells. The
continuous majorants used in the proof of Proposition 14.6 show that the limiting relative measure
has positive mass away from the origin. Its susceptibility and length are therefore strictly positive.
The varying-test argument and the exact cutoff identity (14.25) now prove the normalization
claim. All these inputs concern this shooting prescription itself, so no identification of its auxiliary
parameters with the offset construction is required.


15      Uniqueness after canonical normalization
We now remove the offset parameter. The preceding section gives a continuous family Gs of
canonically normalized hierarchies for s ∈ I = (−R, R), together with its positive second-moment
length ℓ(s) before normalization. Two descriptions of the same microscopic model relate different
members of this family. An additional ordinary step changes the physical mesh by L−1 ; an inclined
first step changes it by 5−1 and rotates it. The two reflected inclinations have the same offset shift.
These facts supply both rotational invariance and two incommensurable periods in s. Figure 3
records the two descriptions and their physical length factors.
     For a rotation U of the Euclidean plane, write U∗ G for the hierarchy obtained by pushing each
insertion coordinate forward by U . At the field level this means (U∗ ϕ)(f ) = ϕ(f ◦ U ). Component
indices are unchanged: these are rotations of Euclidean coordinates, distinct from the internal O(3)
symmetry.

                     inclined

Figure 3: The two scale comparisons. The displayed offsets in the boxes are the bare offsets of the
trajectories; the inclined hierarchy matches the ordinary hierarchy at s + d. Canonical normalization
cancels their different field multipliers. The inclined comparison retains only the Euclidean rotation,
as in (15.2).


15.1    Two descriptions of one cutoff
Proposition 15.1 (Depth and inclination relations). For s, s − γ ∈ I,

                                   Gs−γ = Gs ,       ℓ(s − γ) = L−1 ℓ(s).                        (15.1)

Let d = A+ − Ar = A− − Ar be the offset shift defined in Section 12. For s, s + d ∈ I,
                                                           −1           −1
                       ℓ(s + d) = 5−1 ℓ(s),       Gs+d = (O+  )∗ Gs = (O−  )∗ Gs .               (15.2)

Proof. Fix s, s − γ ∈ I and take bare couplings βN = ϑN + s. Regard the same microscopic law
first as an ordinary trajectory of depth N , and then as one of depth N + 1. The latter offset is

                                 βN − ϑN +1 = s − (ϑN +1 − ϑN ) −→ s − γ.

Both descriptions are admitted for all sufficiently large N by Proposition 12.3. At every cutoff,
canonical normalization in Proposition 14.6 yields the same field ΨβN . Their limiting hierarchies are
therefore equal. Their cutoff second-moment lengths before normalization are respectively L−N ξβN
and L−(N +1) ξβN . Taking their positive limits proves (15.1).
   Next use an ordinary and an inclined trajectory of the same depth N and the same bare coupling
ϑN + s. By Proposition 14.4, the inclined limiting hierarchy is the ordinary one at offset s + d. Its
                                                                 −1
microscopic spacing is L−N /5 and its spatial embedding is O±       . Thus its second-moment length is
exactly one fifth of the ordinary one, while its canonically normalized field is the pushforward of
           −1
ΨβN by O±     . Passing to the limit gives (15.2). Reflection of the microscopic construction identifies
the scalar canonical increments of the two inclinations, which is why the same d occurs in both
equalities.


15.2    An elementary uniqueness mechanism
We isolate the topological part of the argument. Its input consists only of continuous distributions,
positive lengths, and the relations just proved. In particular, it requires no monotonicity of the
shooting map.



                                                     118
Lemma 15.2 (Two scale relations force constancy). Let L > 1 be a power of two, let γ > 0, and
let I = (−R, R). Suppose ℓ : I → (0, ∞) is continuous and s 7→ Gs is a family of distribution
hierarchies whose evaluation on every Schwartz test is continuous. Let O± be rotations by ±ϑ, where
cos ϑ = 3/5 and sin ϑ = 4/5. Suppose (15.1) and (15.2) hold wherever both offsets belong to I, and
suppose 2R − |d| > γ. Then every Gs is Euclidean rotation invariant, and Gs is independent of s.
Proof. First take s in the interval J = I ∩ (I − d). Equality of the two pushforwards in (15.2) implies
invariance of Gs under rotation by 2ϑ. The number ϑ/π is irrational. Indeed, if it were rational,
eiϑ would be a root of unity, making eiϑ + e−iϑ = 6/5 an algebraic integer. A rational algebraic
integer is an integer, which 6/5 is not. Powers of this rotation are consequently dense in SO(2).
The action of rotations on Schwartz tests is continuous, so every distribution in Gs is invariant
under all Euclidean rotations. Equation (15.2) therefore gives Gs+d = Gs on J.
    To use these local relations globally, extend the two families to R. For x ∈ R, choose an integer
n with x + nγ ∈ I and set
                              G
                              e x = Gx+nγ ,      ℓ(x)
                                                 e    = L−n ℓ(x + nγ).                          (15.3)
Such an n exists because |I| > γ. These definitions do not depend on the choice: two representatives
can be joined by consecutive γ-steps staying in the interval I, and (15.1) applies at every step. On
overlapping translates of I, the definitions agree; hence the extended families are continuous. They
satisfy
                                 Ge x+γ = Gex,      ℓ(x
                                                    e + γ) = Lℓ(x).
                                                                e

The interval J has length 2R − |d| > γ, so every class modulo γ has a representative in J. Choosing
that representative in (15.3) transports both the rotation invariance and the d-relations to every
x ∈ R:
                                G
                                e x+d = Gex,     ℓ(x + d) = 5−1 e
                                                 e             ℓ(x).                          (15.4)
   If d/γ = p/q with integers p and q > 0, then iterating the length relations gives
                             5−q ℓ(x)
                                 e    = ℓ(x       e + pγ) = Lp ℓ(x).
                                        e + qd) = ℓ(x          e

Since ℓ(x)
       e    > 0, this would imply 5−q = Lp , contradicting unique prime factorization. Thus d/γ is
irrational. The subgroup Zγ + Zd is dense in R. Each test evaluation of G e is continuous and has
this subgroup as periods, and is therefore constant. Equality on every test proves equality of the
hierarchies. Restriction to I proves the claim.

15.3    Every bare coupling and the physical conclusions
Proof of Theorem 1.1. Finiteness and strict positivity of χβ and ξβ for sufficiently large β follow
from Lemma 13.1. Proposition 15.1 supplies the hypotheses of Lemma 15.2; our choice of R in
Section 12 gives the required overlap. Write G for the common normalized hierarchy.
   The reference values ϑN tend to infinity, and ϑN +1 − ϑN → γ > 0. For every sufficiently large β,
choose N = N (β) so that
                                         ϑN ≤ β < ϑN +1 .
Then N (β) → ∞, and the offsets s(β) = β − ϑN (β) belong to the fixed compact interval [0, 2γ] ⊂
I. Any sequence βk → ∞ has a subsequence on which these offsets converge to some s ∈ I.
Propositions 14.4 and 14.6 give, along that subsequence, convergence of the canonically normalized
fields to G, jointly in law and in every joint moment, and strong convergence of each component
Schwinger distribution in S ′ . Moreover,
                                      L−N (βk ) ξβk −→ ℓ(s) > 0,

                                                 119
so ξβk → ∞ along the same subsequence.
    These conclusions hold for a subsequence of every sequence tending to infinity, with the same
limiting hierarchy. If any asserted convergence failed, a sequence staying outside a fixed neighborhood
of the claimed limit would have such a subsequence, a contradiction. This proves the full β → ∞
assertions. The same argument applies to a neighborhood in the strong topology of S ′ ; it does not
require that topology to be metrizable. The finite-list laws are consistent, and their local exponential
moments determine them from G.
    Finally let ϕsh be the hierarchy in Theorem 1.2, obtained by fixing a terminal kinetic coupling.
Its auxiliary parameters need not equal those used for the offset argument. Lemma 14.7 gives finite
positive susceptibility msh and second-moment length ℓsh , and convergence of the corresponding
cutoff quantities. Thus its normalization
                                                    1
                                  ϕcan (f ) =           ϕsh f ( · /ℓsh )
                                                                        
                                                   √                                             (15.5)
                                                ℓsh msh

is the limit of the same microscopic fields ΨβN along its shooting sequence. Since βN → ∞, this
hierarchy is G. Proposition 14.6 also proves uniqueness of the positive length and field constants
fixing susceptibility and second-moment length to one.

Proof of Corollary 1.3. Equation (15.5) is a positive dilation of space-time and multiplication of the
field by a nonzero constant. It preserves the Euclidean and internal symmetries, reflection positivity,
symmetry of the Schwinger functions, clustering, and their factorial distributional bounds. The
reconstruction argument of Section 11 therefore applies.
     More explicitly, substitution of the rescaled tests and field factors defines an invertible map of
the positive-time polynomial algebras and preserves their Osterwalder–Schrader inner products. It
induces a unitary map between the completed reconstructed spaces, taking vacuum to vacuum. A
time translation by t in canonical coordinates corresponds to one by ℓsh t in shooting coordinates.
Consequently the two Hamiltonians satisfy, under this unitary map,

                                           Hcan = ℓsh Hphys .

The unique vacuum, the nonzero vacuum complement, and the positive lower bound on the full
vacuum-orthogonal Hamiltonian therefore persist. A connected fourth distribution transforms by the
nonzero fourth power of the field factor and the invertible dilation of its tests. Strict separation of
time supports is preserved by a positive dilation. The nonzero separated connected fourth correlation
of Theorem 1.2 thus remains nonzero in canonical units.
